========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I came in because I don’t really trust myself right now. Not in the sense that I’m trying to do something, but more like I feel like I’m this danger walking around, especially at work. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it’s like my brain has decided that it proves something about me — that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I shouldn’t be around patients, that I’m a disappointment to everyone who believed in me.

And I know people might say, you know, “near-misses happen” or “that’s why there are systems,” but I can’t make myself believe that. I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. It feels like I fooled everyone into thinking I could do this job, and now it’s obvious I can’t.

I feel like I’ve let everyone down. My patients, my coworkers, my family… even just being here, I feel embarrassed saying it out loud. But I can’t stop thinking that maybe everyone would be better off if I just wasn’t part of things, if I wasn’t in the way or capable of messing something up. That’s kind of why I came. I don’t know what to do with that feeling anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t have a plan. I’m not sitting here with some specific way I’m going to hurt myself, and I don’t think I’m about to leave here and do something. But I keep having these thoughts, and they scare me because they feel like they keep getting louder when I’m alone.

It’s more like… I’ll think, “It would be easier if I just didn’t wake up,” or “What if I could just disappear?” Not even in a dramatic way, just like… if I wasn’t here, then I couldn’t mess anything up again. I couldn’t hurt anyone, I couldn’t disappoint anyone, I couldn’t be this burden people have to deal with.

So I don’t know if that counts as thinking about killing myself exactly, but I am thinking about not being here. A lot. And I feel ashamed saying that, because part of me knows how it sounds, but that’s what’s been happening.

increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: People at the clinic have asked if I'm tired because I move slower getting supplies and sometimes it takes me a few seconds to answer even simple questions.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I have to read the same medication order or lecture slide over and over because my brain feels foggy, and even small choices like what to chart first can freeze me up.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I keep having thoughts like it would be easier if I didn't wake up or if I could just disappear so I wouldn't mess anything up again.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve fallen behind in my nursing prerequisites and at work I’ve needed coworkers to double-check tasks I used to handle confidently, which makes me afraid to take on normal clinic responsibilities.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, starting after I graduated high school and began college classes and full-time clinic work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 18 / registered_nurse / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I don’t really trust myself right now. Not in the sense that I’m trying to do something, but more like I feel like I’m this danger walking around, especially at work. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it’s like my brain has decided that it proves something about me — that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I shouldn’t be around patients, that I’m a disappointment to everyone who believed in me.\n\nAnd I know people might say, you know, “near-misses happen” or “that’s why there are systems,” but I can’t make myself believe that. I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. It feels like I fooled everyone into thinking I could do this job, and now it’s obvious I can’t.\n\nI feel like I’ve let everyone down. My patients, my coworkers, my family… even just being here, I feel embarrassed saying it out loud. But I can’t stop thinking that maybe everyone would be better off if I just wasn’t part of things, if I wasn’t in the way or capable of messing something up. That’s kind of why I came. I don’t know what to do with that feeling anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t have a plan. But I keep having these thoughts, and they scare me because they feel like they keep getting louder when I’m alone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll think, “It would be easier if I just didn’t wake up,” or “What if I could just disappear?” Not even in a dramatic way, just like… if I wasn’t here, then I couldn’t mess anything up again. I couldn’t hurt anyone, I couldn’t disappoint anyone, I couldn’t be this burden people have to deal with.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as thinking about killing myself exactly, but I am thinking about not being here. A lot. And I feel ashamed saying that, because part of me knows how it sounds, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, most days. It’s there almost as soon as I wake up, this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even remember what I’m supposed to do that day, my body already feels sad.\n\nAnd then I try to go to work and act normal, because I have to. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. Like jogging — I’d go for a run and it would kind of clear my head, like at least my body was doing something and I could breathe for a while. Or I’d knit, which sounds kind of old-lady-ish, I know, but it used to calm me down because it was repetitive and I could make something. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.

Now I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.

Sometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.

I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this depressed most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

It’s been… a long time. I think about a year and a couple months now. Like maybe around fourteen months.

It started after I graduated high school and then went straight into taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.

So… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. Since that transition after graduation.

Like jogging — I’d go for a run and it would kind of clear my head, like at least my body was doing something and I could breathe for a while. Or I’d knit, which sounds kind of old-lady-ish, I know, but it used to calm me down because it was repetitive and I could make something. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed most days?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think about a year and a couple months now. Like maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school and then went straight into taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s changed. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.

And then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.

I’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.

Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 18 / registered_nurse / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"At night I lie there replaying charts and medication calculations, and when I do fall asleep I wake up before my alarm already exhausted and scared I missed something.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.

I’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.

And then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.

I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave other people noticed that you’re moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 18 / registered_nurse / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"People at the clinic have asked if I'm tired because I move slower getting supplies and sometimes it takes me a few seconds to answer even simple questions.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… a little. I didn’t notice it at first, or I kind of didn’t want to notice it, but people at the clinic have said things.

Like someone will ask if I’m tired, not in a mean way, just like, “Are you okay? You seem really tired today.” And I think part of it is because I’m slower getting things, like if I’m grabbing supplies or restocking something, it takes me longer than it should. I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.

And with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. It’s just slower.

You seem really tired today.” And I think part of it is because I’m slower getting things, like if I’m grabbing supplies or restocking something, it takes me longer than it should. I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.\n\nAnd with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. It’s just slower.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that scares me the most, because it’s not just like I’m distracted for a minute. It feels like my brain gets foggy, like I’m looking right at something and it’s not going in.

At work, I’ll read a medication order and then realize I have to read it again… and then again, because I don’t trust that I actually understood it the first time. It’s like my eyes move over the words, but my brain doesn’t hold onto them. And then I get stuck checking myself, because obviously with meds I can’t just be “kind of sure.” So something that should be quick takes me way longer.

It happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and read the same line over and over, and I know it’s basic information I should be able to process, but it just feels like there’s a delay. Like everything has to pass through mud before I understand it.

Even small choices feel harder than they should. Like deciding what to chart first, or what task to do next, I’ll just freeze for a second and kind of stare at the screen. It’s embarrassing because I know I’m capable of doing these things, but lately it feels like my brain won’t organize itself.

I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. Yeah… I have. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from this feeling that I’m… dangerous, I guess. Like not dangerous on purpose, but like I’m someone who could hurt a patient because I’m not careful enough or good enough or stable enough to be doing this.

There was that near-miss, and I know people can say, “It didn’t happen,” or “It was caught,” or whatever, but in my head it doesn’t feel small. It feels like proof. Like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone who believed I could do this was wrong. I keep replaying it and thinking, if I was better, if I was sharper, if I was the kind of person who should be trusted with patients, it wouldn’t have happened at all.

And then it turns into this bigger thing, where I feel like I’m disappointing everyone — my coworkers, my family, my instructors, patients who need someone competent. I feel like I’m taking up space in a role I don’t deserve. Sometimes it feels like everyone would be better off without me there, because then at least they wouldn’t have to worry about whether I’m going to mess something up.

I’m not sitting here with some specific way I’m going to hurt myself, and I don’t think I’m about to leave here and do something. But I keep having these thoughts, and they scare me because they feel like they keep getting louder when I’m alone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll think, “It would be easier if I just didn’t wake up,” or “What if I could just disappear?” Not even in a dramatic way, just like… if I wasn’t here, then I couldn’t mess anything up again. I couldn’t hurt anyone, I couldn’t disappoint anyone, I couldn’t be this burden people have to deal with.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as thinking about killing myself exactly, but I am thinking about not being here. A lot. And I feel ashamed saying that, because part of me knows how it sounds, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, most days. It’s there almost as soon as I wake up, this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even remember what I’m supposed to do that day, my body already feels sad.\n\nAnd then I try to go to work and act normal, because I have to. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. Like jogging — I’d go for a run and it would kind of clear my head, like at least my body was doing something and I could breathe for a while. Or I’d knit, which sounds kind of old-lady-ish, I know, but it used to calm me down because it was repetitive and I could make something. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed most days?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think about a year and a couple months now. Like maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school and then went straight into taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. Since that transition after graduation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’re moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I didn’t notice it at first, or I kind of didn’t want to notice it, but people at the clinic have said things.\n\nLike someone will ask if I’m tired, not in a mean way, just like, “Are you okay? You seem really tired today.” And I think part of it is because I’m slower getting things, like if I’m grabbing supplies or restocking something, it takes me longer than it should. I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.\n\nAnd with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. It’s just slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that scares me the most, because it’s not just like I’m distracted for a minute. It feels like my brain gets foggy, like I’m looking right at something and it’s not going in.\n\nAt work, I’ll read a medication order and then realize I have to read it again… and then again, because I don’t trust that I actually understood it the first time. It’s like my eyes move over the words, but my brain doesn’t hold onto them. And then I get stuck checking myself, because obviously with meds I can’t just be “kind of sure.” So something that should be quick takes me way longer.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and read the same line over and over, and I know it’s basic information I should be able to process, but it just feels like there’s a delay. Like everything has to pass through mud before I understand it.\n\nEven small choices feel harder than they should. Like deciding what to chart first, or what task to do next, I’ll just freeze for a second and kind of stare at the screen. It’s embarrassing because I know I’m capable of doing these things, but lately it feels like my brain won’t organize itself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from this feeling that I’m… dangerous, I guess. Like not dangerous on purpose, but like I’m someone who could hurt a patient because I’m not careful enough or good enough or stable enough to be doing this.\n\nThere was that near-miss, and I know people can say, “It didn’t happen,” or “It was caught,” or whatever, but in my head it doesn’t feel small. It feels like proof. Like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone who believed I could do this was wrong. I keep replaying it and thinking, if I was better, if I was sharper, if I was the kind of person who should be trusted with patients, it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nAnd then it turns into this bigger thing, where I feel like I’m disappointing everyone — my coworkers, my family, my instructors, patients who need someone competent. I feel like I’m taking up space in a role I don’t deserve. Sometimes it feels like everyone would be better off without me there, because then at least they wouldn’t have to worry about whether I’m going to mess something up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not like a voice, no. It’s not like I hear someone outside of me saying it, or like there’s a message telling me I have to be punished. It’s more… it’s my own thoughts, but they get really stuck and loud in my head.

Like I can know, technically, that the near-miss was caught and that people have told me it doesn’t mean I’m a horrible nurse or that I’m unsafe forever. I can hear that, and part of me understands what they’re saying. But emotionally it still feels like proof that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like if I were really meant to do this, if I were actually competent, then it wouldn’t have happened at all.

So I wouldn’t say nobody could talk me out of it completely, because I do have moments where I can kind of see the logical side. But the feeling comes right back — that I’m a danger, that I’m disappointing everyone, that patients would be better off if I wasn’t the one taking care of them. It’s not a fixed belief like I literally know I harmed someone. It’s more this constant guilt that I can’t shake.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.1, F43.22, F41.1, Z73.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I don’t really trust myself right now. Not in the sense that I’m trying to do something, but more like I feel like I’m this danger walking around, especially at work. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it’s like my brain has decided that it proves something about me — that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I shouldn’t be around patients, that I’m a disappointment to everyone who believed in me.\n\nAnd I know people might say, you know, “near-misses happen” or “that’s why there are systems,” but I can’t make myself believe that. I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. It feels like I fooled everyone into thinking I could do this job, and now it’s obvious I can’t.\n\nI feel like I’ve let everyone down. My patients, my coworkers, my family… even just being here, I feel embarrassed saying it out loud. But I can’t stop thinking that maybe everyone would be better off if I just wasn’t part of things, if I wasn’t in the way or capable of messing something up. That’s kind of why I came. I don’t know what to do with that feeling anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t have a plan. I’m not sitting here with some specific way I’m going to hurt myself, and I don’t think I’m about to leave here and do something. But I keep having these thoughts, and they scare me because they feel like they keep getting louder when I’m alone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll think, “It would be easier if I just didn’t wake up,” or “What if I could just disappear?” Not even in a dramatic way, just like… if I wasn’t here, then I couldn’t mess anything up again. I couldn’t hurt anyone, I couldn’t disappoint anyone, I couldn’t be this burden people have to deal with.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as thinking about killing myself exactly, but I am thinking about not being here. A lot. And I feel ashamed saying that, because part of me knows how it sounds, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, most days. It’s there almost as soon as I wake up, this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even remember what I’m supposed to do that day, my body already feels sad.\n\nAnd then I try to go to work and act normal, because I have to. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. Like jogging — I’d go for a run and it would kind of clear my head, like at least my body was doing something and I could breathe for a while. Or I’d knit, which sounds kind of old-lady-ish, I know, but it used to calm me down because it was repetitive and I could make something. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed most days?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think about a year and a couple months now. Like maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school and then went straight into taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. Since that transition after graduation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’re moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I didn’t notice it at first, or I kind of didn’t want to notice it, but people at the clinic have said things.\n\nLike someone will ask if I’m tired, not in a mean way, just like, “Are you okay? You seem really tired today.” And I think part of it is because I’m slower getting things, like if I’m grabbing supplies or restocking something, it takes me longer than it should. I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.\n\nAnd with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. It’s just slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that scares me the most, because it’s not just like I’m distracted for a minute. It feels like my brain gets foggy, like I’m looking right at something and it’s not going in.\n\nAt work, I’ll read a medication order and then realize I have to read it again… and then again, because I don’t trust that I actually understood it the first time. It’s like my eyes move over the words, but my brain doesn’t hold onto them. And then I get stuck checking myself, because obviously with meds I can’t just be “kind of sure.” So something that should be quick takes me way longer.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and read the same line over and over, and I know it’s basic information I should be able to process, but it just feels like there’s a delay. Like everything has to pass through mud before I understand it.\n\nEven small choices feel harder than they should. Like deciding what to chart first, or what task to do next, I’ll just freeze for a second and kind of stare at the screen. It’s embarrassing because I know I’m capable of doing these things, but lately it feels like my brain won’t organize itself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from this feeling that I’m… dangerous, I guess. Like not dangerous on purpose, but like I’m someone who could hurt a patient because I’m not careful enough or good enough or stable enough to be doing this.\n\nThere was that near-miss, and I know people can say, “It didn’t happen,” or “It was caught,” or whatever, but in my head it doesn’t feel small. It feels like proof. Like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone who believed I could do this was wrong. I keep replaying it and thinking, if I was better, if I was sharper, if I was the kind of person who should be trusted with patients, it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nAnd then it turns into this bigger thing, where I feel like I’m disappointing everyone — my coworkers, my family, my instructors, patients who need someone competent. I feel like I’m taking up space in a role I don’t deserve. Sometimes it feels like everyone would be better off without me there, because then at least they wouldn’t have to worry about whether I’m going to mess something up.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're a danger to patients, have you ever heard a voice or had a fixed belief that others couldn't talk you out of telling you that you’ve harmed someone or deserve punishment?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like a voice, no. It’s not like I hear someone outside of me saying it, or like there’s a message telling me I have to be punished. It’s more… it’s my own thoughts, but they get really stuck and loud in my head.\n\nLike I can know, technically, that the near-miss was caught and that people have told me it doesn’t mean I’m a horrible nurse or that I’m unsafe forever. I can hear that, and part of me understands what they’re saying. But emotionally it still feels like proof that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like if I were really meant to do this, if I were actually competent, then it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say nobody could talk me out of it completely, because I do have moments where I can kind of see the logical side. But the feeling comes right back — that I’m a danger, that I’m disappointing everyone, that patients would be better off if I wasn’t the one taking care of them. It’s not a fixed belief like I literally know I harmed someone. It’s more this constant guilt that I can’t shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not for several days or anything like that.

I mean, I get irritable sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual in that way you’re describing. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or tired and then I’m short with people, and afterward I usually feel bad about it. I can’t really think of a time where I was suddenly feeling amazing or euphoric, like I had all this energy or confidence for days.

If anything, when I’ve had “better” moments, they’re pretty brief — like maybe I get through a shift or I have a couple hours where I feel a little more normal, and then it fades. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a stretch of several days where I felt high or unusually up. And the irritability doesn’t feel like a separate episode, if that makes sense.

I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. Since that transition after graduation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’re moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I didn’t notice it at first, or I kind of didn’t want to notice it, but people at the clinic have said things.\n\nLike someone will ask if I’m tired, not in a mean way, just like, “Are you okay? I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.\n\nAnd with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. I’ll look at a lecture slide and read the same line over and over, and I know it’s basic information I should be able to process, but it just feels like there’s a delay. Like everything has to pass through mud before I understand it.\n\nEven small choices feel harder than they should. Like deciding what to chart first, or what task to do next, I’ll just freeze for a second and kind of stare at the screen. It’s embarrassing because I know I’m capable of doing these things, but lately it feels like my brain won’t organize itself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from this feeling that I’m… dangerous, I guess. Like not dangerous on purpose, but like I’m someone who could hurt a patient because I’m not careful enough or good enough or stable enough to be doing this.\n\nThere was that near-miss, and I know people can say, “It didn’t happen,” or “It was caught,” or whatever, but in my head it doesn’t feel small. It feels like proof. Like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone who believed I could do this was wrong. I keep replaying it and thinking, if I was better, if I was sharper, if I was the kind of person who should be trusted with patients, it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nAnd then it turns into this bigger thing, where I feel like I’m disappointing everyone — my coworkers, my family, my instructors, patients who need someone competent. I feel like I’m taking up space in a role I don’t deserve. Sometimes it feels like everyone would be better off without me there, because then at least they wouldn’t have to worry about whether I’m going to mess something up.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're a danger to patients, have you ever heard a voice or had a fixed belief that others couldn't talk you out of telling you that you’ve harmed someone or deserve punishment?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like a voice, no. It’s not like I hear someone outside of me saying it, or like there’s a message telling me I have to be punished. It’s more… it’s my own thoughts, but they get really stuck and loud in my head.\n\nLike I can know, technically, that the near-miss was caught and that people have told me it doesn’t mean I’m a horrible nurse or that I’m unsafe forever. I can hear that, and part of me understands what they’re saying. But emotionally it still feels like proof that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like if I were really meant to do this, if I were actually competent, then it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say nobody could talk me out of it completely, because I do have moments where I can kind of see the logical side. But the feeling comes right back — that I’m a danger, that I’m disappointing everyone, that patients would be better off if I wasn’t the one taking care of them. It’s not a fixed belief like I literally know I harmed someone. It’s more this constant guilt that I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually high, euphoric, or very irritable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for several days or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I get irritable sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual in that way you’re describing. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or tired and then I’m short with people, and afterward I usually feel bad about it. I can’t really think of a time where I was suddenly feeling amazing or euphoric, like I had all this energy or confidence for days.\n\nIf anything, when I’ve had “better” moments, they’re pretty brief — like maybe I get through a shift or I have a couple hours where I feel a little more normal, and then it fades. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I don’t really trust myself right now. Not in the sense that I’m trying to do something, but more like I feel like I’m this danger walking around, especially at work. There was this near-miss, and I keep replaying it over and over, and it’s like my brain has decided that it proves something about me — that I’m not meant to be a nurse, that I shouldn’t be around patients, that I’m a disappointment to everyone who believed in me.\n\nAnd I know people might say, you know, “near-misses happen” or “that’s why there are systems,” but I can’t make myself believe that. I just keep thinking if I were better, more careful, more deserving, it wouldn’t have happened at all. It feels like I fooled everyone into thinking I could do this job, and now it’s obvious I can’t.\n\nI feel like I’ve let everyone down. My patients, my coworkers, my family… even just being here, I feel embarrassed saying it out loud. But I can’t stop thinking that maybe everyone would be better off if I just wasn’t part of things, if I wasn’t in the way or capable of messing something up. That’s kind of why I came. I don’t know what to do with that feeling anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t have a plan. I’m not sitting here with some specific way I’m going to hurt myself, and I don’t think I’m about to leave here and do something. But I keep having these thoughts, and they scare me because they feel like they keep getting louder when I’m alone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll think, “It would be easier if I just didn’t wake up,” or “What if I could just disappear?” Not even in a dramatic way, just like… if I wasn’t here, then I couldn’t mess anything up again. I couldn’t hurt anyone, I couldn’t disappoint anyone, I couldn’t be this burden people have to deal with.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as thinking about killing myself exactly, but I am thinking about not being here. A lot. And I feel ashamed saying that, because part of me knows how it sounds, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, most days. It’s there almost as soon as I wake up, this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even remember what I’m supposed to do that day, my body already feels sad.\n\nAnd then I try to go to work and act normal, because I have to. I’ll be smiling at patients, talking to them in Spanish, trying to sound warm and steady and like I’m fully there with them… and inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m performing being okay, and I can do it for little stretches, but the feeling doesn’t really go away. It just sits underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, even if my day was bad. Like jogging — I’d go for a run and it would kind of clear my head, like at least my body was doing something and I could breathe for a while. Or I’d knit, which sounds kind of old-lady-ish, I know, but it used to calm me down because it was repetitive and I could make something. I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I’ll come home and sit on my bed still in my scrubs, and I’ll look at my running shoes or my knitting stuff or a book sitting there, and I know those are things I used to want to do. I know they’re supposed to help. But I can’t make myself reach for them. It feels like there’s no point, or like it would take too much effort to even pretend I’d enjoy it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Just change clothes, just cook something, just read one page,” and then I just sit there. And then time passes, and I’m still sitting there in the same clothes, not doing anything. It’s like the part of me that used to care about those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed most days?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think about a year and a couple months now. Like maybe around fourteen months.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school and then went straight into taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. Since that transition after graduation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. Charts, whether I documented something right, medication calculations, little things someone said, whether I forgot to double-check something. It’s not even always one clear mistake I’m worried about — it’s more like my brain keeps searching for one. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up before my alarm a lot, already tense, already exhausted, like I’m starting the day behind. Sometimes the first feeling I have when I wake up is this scared feeling, like, “Did I miss something?” before I’m even fully awake. So even if I technically slept for some amount of time, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’re moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I didn’t notice it at first, or I kind of didn’t want to notice it, but people at the clinic have said things.\n\nLike someone will ask if I’m tired, not in a mean way, just like, “Are you okay? You seem really tired today.” And I think part of it is because I’m slower getting things, like if I’m grabbing supplies or restocking something, it takes me longer than it should. I’ll be standing there looking at the shelf like I know what I came for, but my body just feels delayed or something.\n\nAnd with talking too… sometimes someone asks me a simple question, something I should answer immediately, and there’s this pause before I can get the words out. It’s only a few seconds, but I can feel it happening, and then I get embarrassed because I know they’re waiting. It makes me seem like I’m not paying attention, but I am. It’s just slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that scares me the most, because it’s not just like I’m distracted for a minute. It feels like my brain gets foggy, like I’m looking right at something and it’s not going in.\n\nAt work, I’ll read a medication order and then realize I have to read it again… and then again, because I don’t trust that I actually understood it the first time. It’s like my eyes move over the words, but my brain doesn’t hold onto them. And then I get stuck checking myself, because obviously with meds I can’t just be “kind of sure.” So something that should be quick takes me way longer.\n\nIt happens with school stuff too. I’ll look at a lecture slide and read the same line over and over, and I know it’s basic information I should be able to process, but it just feels like there’s a delay. Like everything has to pass through mud before I understand it.\n\nEven small choices feel harder than they should. Like deciding what to chart first, or what task to do next, I’ll just freeze for a second and kind of stare at the screen. It’s embarrassing because I know I’m capable of doing these things, but lately it feels like my brain won’t organize itself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from this feeling that I’m… dangerous, I guess. Like not dangerous on purpose, but like I’m someone who could hurt a patient because I’m not careful enough or good enough or stable enough to be doing this.\n\nThere was that near-miss, and I know people can say, “It didn’t happen,” or “It was caught,” or whatever, but in my head it doesn’t feel small. It feels like proof. Like proof that I’m not meant to be a nurse, and that everyone who believed I could do this was wrong. I keep replaying it and thinking, if I was better, if I was sharper, if I was the kind of person who should be trusted with patients, it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nAnd then it turns into this bigger thing, where I feel like I’m disappointing everyone — my coworkers, my family, my instructors, patients who need someone competent. I feel like I’m taking up space in a role I don’t deserve. Sometimes it feels like everyone would be better off without me there, because then at least they wouldn’t have to worry about whether I’m going to mess something up.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're a danger to patients, have you ever heard a voice or had a fixed belief that others couldn't talk you out of telling you that you’ve harmed someone or deserve punishment?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like a voice, no. It’s not like I hear someone outside of me saying it, or like there’s a message telling me I have to be punished. It’s more… it’s my own thoughts, but they get really stuck and loud in my head.\n\nLike I can know, technically, that the near-miss was caught and that people have told me it doesn’t mean I’m a horrible nurse or that I’m unsafe forever. I can hear that, and part of me understands what they’re saying. But emotionally it still feels like proof that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like if I were really meant to do this, if I were actually competent, then it wouldn’t have happened at all.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say nobody could talk me out of it completely, because I do have moments where I can kind of see the logical side. But the feeling comes right back — that I’m a danger, that I’m disappointing everyone, that patients would be better off if I wasn’t the one taking care of them. It’s not a fixed belief like I literally know I harmed someone. It’s more this constant guilt that I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or longer when you felt unusually high, euphoric, or very irritable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for several days or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I get irritable sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual in that way you’re describing. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or tired and then I’m short with people, and afterward I usually feel bad about it. I can’t really think of a time where I was suddenly feeling amazing or euphoric, like I had all this energy or confidence for days.\n\nIf anything, when I’ve had “better” moments, they’re pretty brief — like maybe I get through a shift or I have a couple hours where I feel a little more normal, and then it fades. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a stretch of several days where I felt high or unusually up. And the irritability doesn’t feel like a separate episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if it’s “unusually” tired or nearly every day in the way you mean.\n\nSome days I feel kind of drained, but I’ve also been trying to tell myself that’s just because work is demanding and I’m probably not handling everything well. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s normal tired from something else. I haven’t really been tracking it. Like, I know there are times where I don’t feel like I have much energy, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s every day or that it’s noticeably different enough.\n\nI guess I’d say I’ve noticed feeling worn down, but I’m not totally sure how much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I liked cooking too, and reading after class when I still had the energy for it.\n\nNow I just… don’t care. I think about a year and a couple months now. At first I told myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting and being dramatic or tired because everything changed so fast. But it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way most days felt, and then I realized months were going by and I was still waking up feeling like this.\n\nSo… yeah, about a year and two months, I’d say. I don’t really feel hungry the way I used to, or I guess I don’t notice it. A lot of days I’ll get all the way to late afternoon or even evening and realize I haven’t actually eaten anything real. Like I’ll have coffee, maybe, and then I just keep going, and it doesn’t occur to me until I feel kind of shaky or my stomach feels weird.\n\nAnd then when I do notice, it’s not like I want a meal. I’ll sort of force myself to eat something small, like crackers or whatever is easy, just because I know I’m supposed to put something in my body. But it feels more like a chore than hunger.\n\nI’ve noticed my work pants are getting loose too, which is… I don’t know, it makes me feel a little uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or anything. I just forget to eat, and then when I remember, I don’t really want much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been bad. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t sleep at all, exactly, but it’s like my brain won’t shut off when I’m trying to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’ll be exhausted, like physically tired, and then the second it’s quiet I start replaying everything from work. I’ll be lying there thinking through doses or trying to remember if I signed something off, and then I check the time and it’s way later than I thought.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. And the irritability doesn’t feel like a separate episode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s normal tired from something else. I haven’t really been tracking it. Like, I know there are times where I don’t feel like I have much energy, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s every day or that it’s noticeably different enough.\n\nI guess I’d say I’ve noticed feeling worn down, but I’m not totally sure how much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, in school, or at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 18 / registered_nurse / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve fallen behind in my nursing prerequisites and at work I’ve needed coworkers to double-check tasks I used to handle confidently, which makes me afraid to take on normal clinic responsibilities.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say I’m still managing, like I’m still showing up and trying to keep everything together. But it’s definitely made things harder.

With school, I’ve fallen behind in my nursing prerequisites. Not in a dramatic “I quit everything” way, but enough that I’m behind on assignments and studying, and then the more behind I get, the harder it is to start again. I’ll sit there knowing I need to do it, and then I just feel stuck and guilty because I’m not keeping up the way I should.

And at work, I’m functioning, but not the way I used to. I’ve been asking coworkers to double-check things that I used to do confidently without even thinking twice. Like tasks that should feel normal for me now suddenly feel risky, and I need someone else to confirm I’m not missing something. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want them to think I can’t do my job.

It’s made me afraid to take on normal clinic responsibilities. Even when something is part of my role, I second-guess myself so much that I hesitate or try to get reassurance first. So yes… it’s affecting work and school, and probably home too in the sense that I bring all of that stress back with me.
